class="infobox-header">Signature</th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Titian,_Tiziano_Vecellio_1488-1576_01_Signature.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/Titian%2C_Tiziano_Vecellio_1488-1576_01_Signature.jpg/150px-Titian%2C_Tiziano_Vecellio_1488-1576_01_Signature.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="37" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/Titian%2C_Tiziano_Vecellio_1488-1576_01_Signature.jpg/225px-Titian%2C_Tiziano_Vecellio_1488-1576_01_Signature.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/Titian%2C_Tiziano_Vecellio_1488-1576_01_Signature.jpg/300px-Titian%2C_Tiziano_Vecellio_1488-1576_01_Signature.jpg 2x" data-file-width="698" data-file-height="172" /></a></span></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Tiziano Vecelli</b> or <b>Vecellio</b> (<small>pronounced&#160;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="it-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Italian" title="Help:IPA/Italian">[titˈtsjaːno veˈtʃɛlljo]</a></span>; <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1488/90</span><sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>&#160;&#8211;&#32;27 August 1576),<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> known in English as <b>Titian</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;t&#39; in &#39;tie&#39;">t</span><span title="/ɪ/: &#39;i&#39; in &#39;kit&#39;">ɪ</span><span title="/ʃ/: &#39;sh&#39; in &#39;shy&#39;">ʃ</span><span title="/ən/: &#39;on&#39; in &#39;button&#39;">ən</span></span>/</a></span></span> <a href="/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key" title="Help:Pronunciation respelling key"><i title="English pronunciation respelling"><span style="font-size:90%">TISH</span>-ən</i></a>), was an <a href="/wiki/Italians" title="Italians">Italian</a><sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> (<a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Venice" title="Republic of Venice">Venetian</a>) <a href="/wiki/Renaissance" title="Renaissance">Renaissance</a> painter of <a href="/wiki/Lombards" title="Lombards">Lombard</a> origin,<sup id="cite_ref-Crowe2011_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Crowe2011-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> considered the most important member of the 16th-century <a href="/wiki/Venetian_school_(art)" class="mw-redirect" title="Venetian school (art)">Venetian school</a>. He was born in <a href="/wiki/Pieve_di_Cadore" title="Pieve di Cadore">Pieve di Cadore</a>, near <a href="/wiki/Belluno" title="Belluno">Belluno</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Wolf_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wolf-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> During his lifetime he was often called <i>da Cadore</i>, 'from <a href="/wiki/Cadore" title="Cadore">Cadore</a>', taken from his native region.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111023_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111023-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Recognized by his contemporaries as "The Sun Amidst Small Stars" (recalling the final line of <a href="/wiki/Dante_Alighieri" title="Dante Alighieri">Dante's</a> <i><a href="/wiki/Paradiso_(Dante)" title="Paradiso (Dante)">Paradiso</a></i>), Titian was one of the most versatile of Italian painters, equally adept with portraits, landscape backgrounds, and mythological and religious subjects. His painting methods, particularly in the application and use of colour, exercised a profound influence not only on painters of the late <a href="/wiki/Italian_Renaissance" title="Italian Renaissance">Italian Renaissance</a>, but on future generations of <a href="/wiki/Art_of_Europe" title="Art of Europe">Western artists</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His career was successful from the start, and he became sought after by patrons, initially from Venice and its possessions, then joined by the north Italian princes, and finally the <a href="/wiki/Habsburg" class="mw-redirect" title="Habsburg">Habsburgs</a> and papacy. Along with <a href="/wiki/Giorgione" title="Giorgione">Giorgione</a>, he is considered a founder of the Venetian School of <a href="/wiki/Italian_Renaissance_painting" title="Italian Renaissance painting">Italian Renaissance painting</a>.
</p><p>During the course of his long life, Titian's artistic manner changed drastically,<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> but he retained a lifelong interest in colour. Although his mature works may not contain the vivid, luminous tints of his early pieces, they are renowned for their loose brushwork and subtlety of tone.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Biography">Biography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Titian&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Biography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_years">Early years</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Titian&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early years">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Titian_-_Portrait_of_a_man_with_a_quilted_sleeve.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Titian_-_Portrait_of_a_man_with_a_quilted_sleeve.jpg/220px-Titian_-_Portrait_of_a_man_with_a_quilted_sleeve.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="268" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Titian_-_Portrait_of_a_man_with_a_quilted_sleeve.jpg/330px-Titian_-_Portrait_of_a_man_with_a_quilted_sleeve.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Titian_-_Portrait_of_a_man_with_a_quilted_sleeve.jpg/440px-Titian_-_Portrait_of_a_man_with_a_quilted_sleeve.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3474" data-file-height="4226" /></a><figcaption><i><a href="/wiki/A_Man_with_a_Quilted_Sleeve" title="A Man with a Quilted Sleeve">A Man with a Quilted Sleeve</a></i>, c.&#160;1509, <a href="/wiki/National_Gallery" title="National Gallery">National Gallery</a>, London</figcaption></figure>
<p>The exact time or date of Titian's birth is uncertain. When he was an old man he claimed in a letter to <a href="/wiki/Philip_II_of_Spain" title="Philip II of Spain">Philip II, King of Spain</a>, to have been born in 1474, but this seems most unlikely.<sup id="cite_ref-NG_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NG-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Other writers contemporary to his old age give figures that would equate to birthdates between 1473 and after 1482.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Most modern scholars believe a date between 1488 and 1490 is more likely,<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> though his age at death being 99 had been accepted into the 20th century.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He was the son of Gregorio Vecellio and his wife Lucia, of whom little is known. Gregorio was superintendent of the castle of Pieve di Cadore and managed local mines for their owners.<sup id="cite_ref-DJ_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DJ-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Gregorio was also a distinguished councilor and soldier. Many relatives, including Titian's grandfather, were <a href="/wiki/Civil_law_notary" title="Civil law notary">notaries</a>, and the family was well-established in the area, which was ruled by Venice.
</p><p>At the age of about ten to twelve Titian and his brother Francesco (who perhaps followed later) were sent to an uncle in Venice to find an apprenticeship with a painter. The minor painter Sebastian Zuccato, whose sons became well-known <a href="/wiki/Mosaic" title="Mosaic">mosaicists</a>, and who may have been a family friend, arranged for the brothers to enter the studio of the elderly <a href="/wiki/Gentile_Bellini" title="Gentile Bellini">Gentile Bellini</a>, from which they later transferred to that of his brother <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Bellini" title="Giovanni Bellini">Giovanni Bellini</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-DJ_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DJ-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> At that time the Bellinis, especially Giovanni, were the leading artists in the city. There Titian found a group of young men about his own age, among them Giovanni Palma da Serinalta, <a href="/wiki/Lorenzo_Lotto" title="Lorenzo Lotto">Lorenzo Lotto</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sebastiano_del_Piombo" title="Sebastiano del Piombo">Sebastiano Luciani</a>, and Giorgio da Castelfranco, nicknamed <a href="/wiki/Giorgione" title="Giorgione">Giorgione</a>. <a href="/wiki/Francesco_Vecellio" title="Francesco Vecellio">Francesco Vecellio</a>, Titian's older brother, later became a painter of some note in Venice.
</p><p>A <a href="/wiki/Fresco" title="Fresco">fresco</a> of <a href="/wiki/Hercules" title="Hercules">Hercules</a> on the Morosini Palace is said to have been one of Titian's earliest works.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111023_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111023-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Others were the Bellini-esque so-called <i><a href="/wiki/The_Gypsy_Madonna" title="The Gypsy Madonna">Gypsy Madonna</a></i> in Vienna,<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> and the <i>Visitation of Mary and Elizabeth</i> (from the convent of Sant'Andrea), now in the <a href="/wiki/Gallerie_dell%27Accademia" title="Gallerie dell&#39;Accademia">Accademia</a>, Venice.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111023_7-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111023-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><i><a href="/wiki/A_Man_with_a_Quilted_Sleeve" title="A Man with a Quilted Sleeve">A Man with a Quilted Sleeve</a></i> is an early portrait, painted around 1509 and described by <a href="/wiki/Giorgio_Vasari" title="Giorgio Vasari">Giorgio Vasari</a> in 1568. Scholars long believed it depicted <a href="/wiki/Ludovico_Ariosto" title="Ludovico Ariosto">Ludovico Ariosto</a>, but now think it is of Gerolamo Barbarigo.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Rembrandt" title="Rembrandt">Rembrandt</a> borrowed the composition for his self-portraits.
</p><p>Titian joined Giorgione as an assistant, but many contemporary critics already found Titian's work more impressive—for example, in exterior frescoes (now almost totally destroyed) that they collaborated on for the <a href="/wiki/Fondaco_dei_Tedeschi" title="Fondaco dei Tedeschi">Fondaco dei Tedeschi</a> (state-warehouse for the German merchants).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111023_7-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111023-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Their relationship evidently contained a significant element of rivalry. Distinguishing between their work during this period remains a subject of scholarly controversy. A substantial number of attributions have moved from Giorgione to Titian in the 20th century, with little traffic the other way. One of the earliest known Titian works, <i><a href="/wiki/Christ_Carrying_the_Cross_(Titian)" title="Christ Carrying the Cross (Titian)">Christ Carrying the Cross</a></i> in the <a href="/wiki/Scuola_Grande_di_San_Rocco" title="Scuola Grande di San Rocco">Scuola Grande di San Rocco</a>, depicting the <i><a href="/wiki/Ecce_homo" title="Ecce homo">Ecce Homo</a></i> scene,<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> was long regarded as by Giorgione.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tizian_029.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Tizian_029.jpg/220px-Tizian_029.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="76" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Tizian_029.jpg/330px-Tizian_029.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Tizian_029.jpg/440px-Tizian_029.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1452" data-file-height="499" /></a><figcaption><i><a href="/wiki/Sacred_and_Profane_Love" title="Sacred and Profane Love">Sacred and Profane Love</a>,</i>1514, <a href="/wiki/Galleria_Borghese" title="Galleria Borghese">Galleria Borghese</a>, Rome</figcaption></figure>
<p>The two young masters were likewise recognized as the leaders of their new school of <i>arte moderna</i>, which is characterized by paintings made more flexible, freed from symmetry and the remnants of hieratic conventions still found in the works of Giovanni Bellini.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (March 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In 1507–1508, Giorgione was commissioned by the state to create frescoes on the re-erected Fondaco dei Tedeschi. Titian and <a href="/wiki/Morto_da_Feltre" title="Morto da Feltre">Morto da Feltre</a> worked along with him, and some fragments of paintings remain, probably by Giorgione.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111023_7-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111023-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Some of their work is known, in part, through the engravings of <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Battista_Fontana_(painter)" title="Giovanni Battista Fontana (painter)">Fontana</a>. After Giorgione's early death in 1510, Titian continued to paint Giorgionesque subjects for some time, though his style developed its own features, including bold and expressive brushwork.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Amor_sacro_e_amor_profano_03.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Amor_sacro_e_amor_profano_03.jpg/220px-Amor_sacro_e_amor_profano_03.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="259" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Amor_sacro_e_amor_profano_03.jpg/330px-Amor_sacro_e_amor_profano_03.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Amor_sacro_e_amor_profano_03.jpg/440px-Amor_sacro_e_amor_profano_03.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="1175" /></a><figcaption>Allegory of Profane Love (detail of <i><a href="/wiki/Sacred_and_Profane_Love" title="Sacred and Profane Love">Sacred and Profane Love</a></i>)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Titian's talent in fresco is shown in those he painted in 1511 at <a href="/wiki/Padua" title="Padua">Padua</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Carmelites" title="Carmelites">Carmelite</a> church and in the <a href="https://it.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scuola_del_Santo" class="extiw" title="it:Scuola del Santo">Scuola del Santo</a>, some of which have been preserved, among them the <i>Meeting at the Golden Gate</i>, and three scenes (<i>Miracoli di sant'Antonio</i>) from the life of St. <a href="/wiki/Anthony_of_Padua" title="Anthony of Padua">Anthony of Padua</a>, The Miracle of the Jealous Husband, which depicts the <i>Murder of a Young Woman by Her Husband</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> <i>A Child Testifying to Its Mother's Innocence</i>, and <i>The Saint Healing the Young Man with a Broken Limb</i>. <i><a href="/wiki/The_Resurrected_Christ" title="The Resurrected Christ">The Resurrected Christ</a></i> (Uffizi) also dates to 1511-1512.
</p><p>In 1512 Titian returned to Venice from Padua; in 1513 he obtained <i>La Senseria</i> (a profitable privilege much coveted by artists) in the Fondaco dei Tedeschi.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> He became superintendent of the government works, especially charged with completing the paintings left unfinished by Giovanni Bellini in the hall of the great council in the <a href="/wiki/Doge%27s_Palace,_Venice" class="mw-redirect" title="Doge&#39;s Palace, Venice">ducal palace</a>. He set up an atelier on the <a href="/wiki/Grand_Canal_(Venice)" title="Grand Canal (Venice)">Grand Canal</a> at S. Samuele, the precise site being now unknown. It was not until 1516, after the death of Giovanni Bellini, that he came into actual enjoyment of his patent. At the same time he entered an exclusive arrangement for painting. The patent yielded him a good annuity of 20 crowns and exempted him from certain taxes. In return, he was bound to paint likenesses of the successive <a href="/wiki/Doge_of_Venice" title="Doge of Venice">Doges</a> of his time at the fixed price of eight crowns each. The actual number he painted was five.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111023_7-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111023-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Growth">Growth</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Titian&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Growth">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tizian_041.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9e/Tizian_041.jpg/220px-Tizian_041.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="405" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9e/Tizian_041.jpg/330px-Tizian_041.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9e/Tizian_041.jpg/440px-Tizian_041.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1050" data-file-height="1932" /></a><figcaption><i><a href="/wiki/Assumption_of_the_Virgin_(Titian)" title="Assumption of the Virgin (Titian)">Assumption of the Virgin</a></i>, 1516–1518; it took Titian more than two years to complete this painting in the <a href="/wiki/Santa_Maria_Gloriosa_dei_Frari" title="Santa Maria Gloriosa dei Frari">Frari</a> church in Venice. Its dynamic three-tier composition and colour scheme established him as the preeminent painter north of Rome.</figcaption></figure>
<p>During this period (1516–1530), which may be called the period of his mastery and maturity, the artist moved on from his early <a href="/wiki/Giorgione" title="Giorgione">Giorgionesque</a> style, undertook larger, more complex subjects, and for the first time attempted a monumental style. Giorgione died in 1510 and <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Bellini" title="Giovanni Bellini">Giovanni Bellini</a> in 1516, leaving Titian unrivaled in the Venetian School. For sixty years he was the undisputed master of Venetian painting. In 1516, he completed his famous masterpiece, the <i><a href="/wiki/Assumption_of_the_Virgin_(Titian)" title="Assumption of the Virgin (Titian)">Assumption of the Virgin</a></i>, for the high altar of the <a href="/wiki/Santa_Maria_Gloriosa_dei_Frari" title="Santa Maria Gloriosa dei Frari">Basilica di Santa Maria Gloriosa dei Frari</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111023_7-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111023-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> where it is still <i>in situ</i>. This piece of colourism, executed on a grand scale rarely before seen in Italy, created a sensation.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Signoria" title="Signoria">Signoria</a> took note and observed that Titian was neglecting his work in the hall of the great council,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111023_7-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111023-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> but in 1516 he succeeded his master Giovanni Bellini in receiving a pension from the Senate.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The pictorial structure of the <i>Assumption</i>—that of uniting in the same composition two or three scenes superimposed on different levels, earth and heaven, the temporal and the infinite—was continued in a series of works such as the retable of San Domenico at <a href="/wiki/Ancona" title="Ancona">Ancona</a> (1520), the retable of <a href="/wiki/Brescia" title="Brescia">Brescia</a> (1522), and the retable of San Niccolò (1523), in the <a href="/wiki/Vatican_Museums" title="Vatican Museums">Vatican Museums</a>, each time attaining to a higher and more perfect conception. He finally reached a classic formula in the <i><a href="/wiki/Pesaro_Madonna" title="Pesaro Madonna">Pesaro Madonna</a></i>, better known as the Madonna di Ca' Pesaro (c.&#160;1519–1526), also for the Frari church. This is perhaps his most studied work, whose patiently developed plan is set forth with supreme display of order and freedom, originality and style. Here Titian gave a new conception of the traditional groups of donors and holy persons moving in aerial space, the plans and different degrees set in an architectural framework.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Titian was then at the height of his fame, and towards 1521, following the production of a figure of St. Sebastian for the papal legate in Brescia (of which there are numerous replicas), purchasers pressed for his work.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111023_7-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111023-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>To this period belongs a more extraordinary work, <i><a href="/wiki/The_Assassination_of_Saint_Peter_Martyr_(Titian)" title="The Assassination of Saint Peter Martyr (Titian)">The Assassination of Saint Peter Martyr</a></i> (1530), formerly in the Dominican Church of <a href="/wiki/Santi_Giovanni_e_Paolo,_Venice" title="Santi Giovanni e Paolo, Venice">San Zanipolo</a>, and destroyed by an Austrian shell in 1867. Only copies and <a href="/wiki/Engraving" title="Engraving">engravings</a> of this proto-<a href="/wiki/Baroque" title="Baroque">Baroque</a> picture remain. It combined extreme violence and a landscape, mostly consisting of a great tree, that pressed into the scene and seems to accentuate the drama in a way that looks forward to the Baroque.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The artist simultaneously continued a series of small <a href="/wiki/Madonna_(art)" title="Madonna (art)">Madonnas</a>, which he placed amid beautiful landscapes, in the manner of genre pictures or poetic pastorals. The <i>Virgin with the Rabbit</i>, in <a href="/wiki/The_Louvre" class="mw-redirect" title="The Louvre">The Louvre</a>, is the finished type of these pictures. Another work of the same period, also in the <a href="/wiki/Louvre" title="Louvre">Louvre</a>, is the <i>Entombment</i>. This was also the period of the three large and famous mythological scenes for the <i><a href="/wiki/Cabinet_(room)" title="Cabinet (room)">camerino</a></i> of <a href="/wiki/Alfonso_I_d%27Este,_Duke_of_Ferrara" title="Alfonso I d&#39;Este, Duke of Ferrara">Alfonso d'Este</a> in <a href="/wiki/Ferrara" title="Ferrara">Ferrara</a>, <i><a href="/wiki/The_Bacchanal_of_the_Andrians" title="The Bacchanal of the Andrians">The Bacchanal of the Andrians</a></i> and the <i>Worship of Venus</i> in the <span title="Spanish-language text"><span lang="es" style="font-style: normal;"><a href="/wiki/Museo_del_Prado" title="Museo del Prado">Museo del Prado</a></span></span>, and the <i><a href="/wiki/Bacchus_and_Ariadne" title="Bacchus and Ariadne">Bacchus and Ariadne</a></i> (1520–23) in <a href="/wiki/London" title="London">London</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> "perhaps the most brilliant productions of the neo-pagan culture or "Alexandrianism" of the <a href="/wiki/Renaissance" title="Renaissance">Renaissance</a>, many times imitated but never surpassed even by <a href="/wiki/Peter_Paul_Rubens" title="Peter Paul Rubens">Rubens</a> himself."<sup id="cite_ref-catencyclo_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-catencyclo-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Vecelli,_Tiziano_-_Judith_-_c._1515.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Vecelli%2C_Tiziano_-_Judith_-_c._1515.jpg/220px-Vecelli%2C_Tiziano_-_Judith_-_c._1515.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="268" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Vecelli%2C_Tiziano_-_Judith_-_c._1515.jpg/330px-Vecelli%2C_Tiziano_-_Judith_-_c._1515.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Vecelli%2C_Tiziano_-_Judith_-_c._1515.jpg/440px-Vecelli%2C_Tiziano_-_Judith_-_c._1515.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1066" data-file-height="1300" /></a><figcaption><i><a href="/wiki/Salome_(Titian,_Rome)" title="Salome (Titian, Rome)">Salome with the Head of John the Baptist</a></i>, c.&#160;1515, or <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Judith" title="Book of Judith"><i>Judith</i></a>; this religious work also functions as an idealized portrait of a beauty, a genre developed by Titian, supposedly often using Venetian <a href="/wiki/Courtesan" title="Courtesan">courtesans</a> as models. <a href="/wiki/Galleria_Doria_Pamphilj" class="mw-redirect" title="Galleria Doria Pamphilj">Galleria Doria Pamphilj</a>, Rome.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Finally this was the period when Titian composed the half-length figures and busts of young women, such as <i><a href="/wiki/Flora_(Titian)" title="Flora (Titian)">Flora</a></i> in the <a href="/wiki/Uffizi" title="Uffizi">Uffizi</a> and <i><a href="/wiki/Woman_with_a_Mirror" title="Woman with a Mirror">Woman with a Mirror</a></i> in the Louvre. At least according to popular legend, they were modeled by some of Venice's famous <a href="/wiki/Courtesan" title="Courtesan">courtesans</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Maturity">Maturity</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Titian&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Maturity">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Titian's skill with colour is exemplified by his <i><a href="/wiki/Dana%C3%AB_(Titian_series)" class="mw-redirect" title="Danaë (Titian series)">Danaë</a></i>, one of several mythological paintings, or "poesie" ("poems") as the painter called them. This painting was done for Alessandro Farnese, but a later variant was produced for Philip II, for whom Titian painted many of his most important mythological paintings. Although Michelangelo adjudged this piece deficient from the point of view of drawing, Titian and his studio produced several versions for other patrons.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Titian_Bacchus_and_Ariadne.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/Titian_Bacchus_and_Ariadne.jpg/270px-Titian_Bacchus_and_Ariadne.jpg" decoding="async" width="270" height="244" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/Titian_Bacchus_and_Ariadne.jpg/405px-Titian_Bacchus_and_Ariadne.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/Titian_Bacchus_and_Ariadne.jpg/540px-Titian_Bacchus_and_Ariadne.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4670" data-file-height="4226" /></a><figcaption><i><a href="/wiki/Bacchus_and_Ariadne" title="Bacchus and Ariadne">Bacchus and Ariadne</a></i>, c.&#160;1520–1523. <a href="/wiki/National_Gallery" title="National Gallery">National Gallery</a>, London.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Another famous painting is <i><a href="/wiki/Bacchus_and_Ariadne" title="Bacchus and Ariadne">Bacchus and Ariadne</a></i>, depicting <a href="/wiki/Theseus" title="Theseus">Theseus</a>, whose ship is shown in the distance and who has just left Ariadne at Naxos, when Bacchus arrives, jumping from his chariot, drawn by two cheetahs, and falling immediately in love with Ariadne. Bacchus raised her to heaven. Her constellation is shown in the sky. The painting belongs to a series commissioned from Bellini, Titian, and Dosso Dossi, for the <a href="/wiki/Camerino_d%27Alabastro" class="mw-redirect" title="Camerino d&#39;Alabastro">Camerino d'Alabastro</a> (Alabaster Room) in the Ducal Palace, <a href="/wiki/Ferrara" title="Ferrara">Ferrara</a>, by <a href="/wiki/Alfonso_I_d%27Este,_Duke_of_Ferrara" title="Alfonso I d&#39;Este, Duke of Ferrara">Alfonso I d'Este, Duke of Ferrara</a>, who in 1510 even tried to commission <a href="/wiki/Michelangelo" title="Michelangelo">Michelangelo</a> and <a href="/wiki/Raphael" title="Raphael">Raphael</a>.
</p><p>During the next period (1530–1550), Titian developed the style introduced by his dramatic <i>Death of St. Peter Martyr</i>. In 1538, the Venetian government, dissatisfied with Titian's neglect of his work for the ducal palace, ordered him to refund the money he had received, and <a href="/wiki/Il_Pordenone" title="Il Pordenone">Il Pordenone</a>, his rival of recent years, was installed in his place. However, at the end of a year Pordenone died, and Titian, who meanwhile applied himself diligently to painting in the hall the <i>Battle of Cadore</i>, was reinstated.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111024_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111024-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>This major battle scene was lost—with many other major works by Venetian artists—in the 1577 fire that destroyed all the old pictures in the great chambers of the Doge's Palace. It depicted in life-size the moment when the Venetian general <a href="/wiki/Bartolomeo_d%27Alviano" title="Bartolomeo d&#39;Alviano">d'Alviano</a> attacked the enemy, with horses and men crashing down into a stream.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111024_27-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111024-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> It was Titian's most important attempt at a tumultuous and heroic scene of movement to rival <a href="/wiki/Raphael" title="Raphael">Raphael</a>'s <i>Battle of Constantine</i>, Michelangelo's equally ill-fated <i>Battle of Cascina</i>, and <a href="/wiki/Leonardo_da_Vinci" title="Leonardo da Vinci">Leonardo da Vinci</a>'s <i>The Battle of Anghiari</i> (these last two unfinished). There remains only a poor, incomplete copy at the Uffizi, and a mediocre engraving by Fontana. The <i>Speech of the Marquis del Vasto</i> (Madrid, 1541) was also partly destroyed by fire. But this period of the master's work is still represented by the <i>Presentation of the Blessed Virgin</i> (Venice, 1539), one of his most popular canvasses, and by the <i>Ecce Homo</i> (<a href="/wiki/Vienna" title="Vienna">Vienna</a>, 1541). Despite its loss, the painting had a great influence on <a href="/wiki/Bologna" title="Bologna">Bolognese</a> art and Rubens, both in the handling of details and the general effect of horses, soldiers, lictors, powerful stirrings of crowds at the foot of a stairway, lit by torches with the flapping of banners against the sky.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tizian_-_Portrait_of_Federico_II_Gonzaga_-_circa_1525.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/11/Tizian_-_Portrait_of_Federico_II_Gonzaga_-_circa_1525.jpg/220px-Tizian_-_Portrait_of_Federico_II_Gonzaga_-_circa_1525.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="279" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/11/Tizian_-_Portrait_of_Federico_II_Gonzaga_-_circa_1525.jpg/330px-Tizian_-_Portrait_of_Federico_II_Gonzaga_-_circa_1525.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/11/Tizian_-_Portrait_of_Federico_II_Gonzaga_-_circa_1525.jpg/440px-Tizian_-_Portrait_of_Federico_II_Gonzaga_-_circa_1525.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2405" data-file-height="3051" /></a><figcaption><i><a href="/wiki/Portrait_of_Federico_II_Gonzaga" title="Portrait of Federico II Gonzaga">Portrait of Federico II Gonzaga</a></i>, c.&#160;1529. <span title="Spanish-language text"><span lang="es" style="font-style: normal;"><a href="/wiki/Museo_del_Prado" title="Museo del Prado">Museo del Prado</a></span></span>, Madrid.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Less successful were the <a href="/wiki/Pendentive" title="Pendentive">pendentives</a> of the cupola at <a href="/wiki/Santa_Maria_della_Salute" title="Santa Maria della Salute">Santa Maria della Salute</a> (<i>Death of Abel</i>, <i>Sacrifice of Abraham</i>, <i>David and Goliath</i>). These violent scenes viewed in perspective from below were by their very nature in unfavourable situations. They were nevertheless much admired and imitated, Rubens among others applying this system to his forty ceilings (the sketches only remain) of the <a href="/wiki/Society_of_Jesus" class="mw-redirect" title="Society of Jesus">Jesuit</a> church at Antwerp.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tizian_039.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Tizian_039.jpg/220px-Tizian_039.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="307" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Tizian_039.jpg/330px-Tizian_039.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Tizian_039.jpg/440px-Tizian_039.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="1395" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Pesaro_Madonna" title="Pesaro Madonna">Pesaro altarpiece</a>, 1521–26, <a href="/wiki/Santa_Maria_Gloriosa_dei_Frari" title="Santa Maria Gloriosa dei Frari">Santa Maria Gloriosa dei Frari</a>, Venice</figcaption></figure>
<p>At this time also, during his visit to <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a>, the artist began a series of reclining Venuses: <i>The <a href="/wiki/Venus_of_Urbino" title="Venus of Urbino">Venus of Urbino</a></i> of the Uffizi, <i>Venus and Love</i> at the same museum, <i><a href="/wiki/Venus_and_Music" class="mw-redirect" title="Venus and Music">Venus—and the Organ-Player</a></i>, Madrid, which shows the influence of contact with ancient sculpture. <a href="/wiki/Giorgione" title="Giorgione">Giorgione</a> had already dealt with the subject in his Dresden picture, finished by Titian, but here a purple drapery substituted for a landscape background changed, by its harmonious colouring, the whole meaning of the scene.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Carlos_V_en_M%C3%BChlberg,_by_Titian,_from_Prado_in_Google_Earth.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Carlos_V_en_M%C3%BChlberg%2C_by_Titian%2C_from_Prado_in_Google_Earth.jpg/220px-Carlos_V_en_M%C3%BChlberg%2C_by_Titian%2C_from_Prado_in_Google_Earth.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="261" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Carlos_V_en_M%C3%BChlberg%2C_by_Titian%2C_from_Prado_in_Google_Earth.jpg/330px-Carlos_V_en_M%C3%BChlberg%2C_by_Titian%2C_from_Prado_in_Google_Earth.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Carlos_V_en_M%C3%BChlberg%2C_by_Titian%2C_from_Prado_in_Google_Earth.jpg/440px-Carlos_V_en_M%C3%BChlberg%2C_by_Titian%2C_from_Prado_in_Google_Earth.jpg 2x" data-file-width="25284" data-file-height="30000" /></a><figcaption><i><a href="/wiki/Equestrian_Portrait_of_Charles_V" title="Equestrian Portrait of Charles V">Equestrian Portrait of Charles V</a></i>, 1548, <span title="Spanish-language text"><span lang="es" style="font-style: normal;"><a href="/wiki/Museo_del_Prado" title="Museo del Prado">Museo del Prado</a></span></span></figcaption></figure>
<p>From the beginning of his career, Titian was a masterful portrait-painter, in works like <i>La Bella</i> (Eleanora de Gonzaga, Duchess of Urbino, at the <a href="/wiki/Palazzo_Pitti" title="Palazzo Pitti">Pitti Palace</a>). He painted the likenesses of princes, or Doges, cardinals or monks, and artists or writers. "...no other painter was so successful in extracting from each physiognomy so many traits at once characteristic and beautiful".<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Among portrait-painters Titian is compared to <a href="/wiki/Rembrandt" title="Rembrandt">Rembrandt</a> and <a href="/wiki/Diego_Vel%C3%A1zquez" title="Diego Velázquez">Velázquez</a>, with the interior life of the former, and the clearness, certainty, and obviousness of the latter.
</p><p>These qualities show in the <i><a href="/wiki/Portrait_of_Pope_Paul_III_(Titian)" title="Portrait of Pope Paul III (Titian)">Portrait of Pope Paul III</a></i> of <a href="/wiki/Naples" title="Naples">Naples</a>, or the sketch of the same <i><a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_III_and_his_Grandsons" class="mw-redirect" title="Pope Paul III and his Grandsons">Pope Paul III and his Grandsons</a></i>, the <i><a href="/wiki/Portrait_of_Pietro_Aretino" title="Portrait of Pietro Aretino">Portrait of Pietro Aretino</a></i> of the Pitti Palace, the <i><a href="/wiki/Portrait_of_Isabella_of_Portugal" title="Portrait of Isabella of Portugal">Portrait of Isabella of Portugal</a></i> (Madrid), and the series of <a href="/wiki/Charles_V,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Charles V, Holy Roman Emperor">Emperor Charles V</a> of the same museum, the <i>Charles V with a Greyhound</i> (1533), and especially the <i><a href="/wiki/Equestrian_Portrait_of_Charles_V" title="Equestrian Portrait of Charles V">Equestrian Portrait of Charles V</a></i> (1548), an equestrian picture in a symphony of purples. This state portrait of Charles V (1548) at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_M%C3%BChlberg" title="Battle of Mühlberg">Battle of Mühlberg</a> established a new genre, that of the grand equestrian portrait. The composition is steeped both in the Roman tradition of <a href="/wiki/Equestrian_statue" title="Equestrian statue">equestrian sculpture</a> and in the medieval representations of an ideal Christian knight, but the weary figure and face have a subtlety few such representations attempt. In 1532, after painting a portrait of the emperor Charles V in Bologna, he was made a Count Palatine and <a href="/wiki/Knight_of_the_Golden_Spur_(HRE)" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight of the Golden Spur (HRE)">knight of the Golden Spur</a>. His children were also made nobles of the Empire, which for a painter was an exceptional honor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111024_27-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111024-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Self-portrait_of_Titian.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Self-portrait_of_Titian.jpg/220px-Self-portrait_of_Titian.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Self-portrait_of_Titian.jpg/330px-Self-portrait_of_Titian.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Self-portrait_of_Titian.jpg/440px-Self-portrait_of_Titian.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4524" data-file-height="6018" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Self-Portrait_(Titian,_Berlin)" title="Self-Portrait (Titian, Berlin)"><i>Self-Portrait</i></a>, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1546–47</span></figcaption></figure>
<p>This appointment allowed him to gain royal patronage and work on prestigious commissions.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
As a matter of professional and worldly success, his position from about this time is regarded as equal only to that of <a href="/wiki/Raphael" title="Raphael">Raphael</a>, <a href="/wiki/Michelangelo" title="Michelangelo">Michelangelo</a> and, at a later date, Rubens. In 1540 he received a pension from d'Avalos, marquis del Vasto, and an annuity of 200 crowns (which was afterwards doubled) from Charles V from the treasury of <a href="/wiki/Milan" title="Milan">Milan</a>. Another source of profit, for he was always aware of money, was a contract obtained in 1542 for supplying grain to Cadore, where he visited almost every year and where he was both generous and influential.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111024_27-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111024-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Titian had a favourite villa on the neighboring Manza Hill (in front of the church of <a href="/wiki/Castello_Roganzuolo" title="Castello Roganzuolo">Castello Roganzuolo</a>) from which (it may be inferred) he made his chief observations of landscape form and effect. The so-called Titian's mill, constantly discernible in his studies, is at Collontola, near Belluno.<sup id="cite_ref-heathp5_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-heathp5-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111024_27-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111024-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He visited Rome in 1546 and obtained the freedom of the city—his immediate predecessor in that honor having been <a href="/wiki/Michelangelo" title="Michelangelo">Michelangelo</a> in 1537. He could at the same time have succeeded the painter <a href="/wiki/Sebastiano_del_Piombo" title="Sebastiano del Piombo">Sebastiano del Piombo</a> in his lucrative office as holder of the piombo or Papal <a href="/wiki/Seal_(emblem)" title="Seal (emblem)">seal</a>, and he was prepared to take <a href="/wiki/Holy_Orders" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy Orders">Holy Orders</a> for the purpose; but the project lapsed through his being summoned away from Venice in 1547 to paint Charles V and others in <a href="/wiki/Augsburg" title="Augsburg">Augsburg</a>. He was there again in 1550, and executed the portrait of <a href="/wiki/Philip_II_of_Spain" title="Philip II of Spain">Philip II</a>, which was sent to England and was useful in Philip's suit for the hand of <a href="/wiki/Mary_I_of_England" title="Mary I of England">Queen Mary</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111024_27-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111024-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Final_years">Final years</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Titian&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Final years">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Venus_and_organist_and_little_dog.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Venus_and_organist_and_little_dog.jpg/280px-Venus_and_organist_and_little_dog.jpg" decoding="async" width="280" height="169" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Venus_and_organist_and_little_dog.jpg/420px-Venus_and_organist_and_little_dog.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Venus_and_organist_and_little_dog.jpg/560px-Venus_and_organist_and_little_dog.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3051" data-file-height="1837" /></a><figcaption><i><a href="/wiki/Venus_and_Musician" title="Venus and Musician">Venus and Organist and Little Dog</a></i>, c.&#160;1550. <span title="Spanish-language text"><span lang="es" style="font-style: normal;"><a href="/wiki/Museo_del_Prado" title="Museo del Prado">Museo del Prado</a></span></span>, Madrid.</figcaption></figure>
<p>During the last twenty-six years of his life (1550–1576), Titian worked mainly for Philip II and as a portrait-painter. He became more self-critical, an insatiable perfectionist, keeping some pictures in his studio for ten years—returning to them and retouching them, constantly adding new expressions at once more refined, concise, and subtle. He also finished many copies that his pupils made of his earlier works. This caused problems of attribution and priority among versions of his works—which were also widely copied and faked outside his studio during his lifetime and afterwards.
</p><p>For Philip II, he painted a series of large mythological paintings known as the "poesie", mostly from <a href="/wiki/Ovid" title="Ovid">Ovid</a>, which scholars regard as among his greatest works.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Thanks to the prudishness of Philip's successors, these were later mostly given as gifts, and only two remain in the Prado. Titian was producing religious works for Philip at the same time, some of which—the ones inside <a href="/wiki/Ribeira_Palace" title="Ribeira Palace">Ribeira Palace</a>—are known to have been destroyed during the <a href="/wiki/1755_Lisbon_earthquake" title="1755 Lisbon earthquake">1755 Lisbon Earthquake</a>. The "poesie" series contained the following works:
</p>
<ul><li><i><a href="/wiki/Dana%C3%AB_(Titian_series)" class="mw-redirect" title="Danaë (Titian series)">Danaë</a></i>, sent to Philip in 1553,<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> now in the Wellington Collection, with earlier and later versions</li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/Venus_and_Adonis_(Titian)" title="Venus and Adonis (Titian)">Venus and Adonis</a></i>, of which the earliest surviving version, delivered in 1554, is in the Prado, but several versions exist</li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/Perseus_and_Andromeda_(Titian)" title="Perseus and Andromeda (Titian)">Perseus and Andromeda</a></i> (<a href="/wiki/Wallace_Collection" title="Wallace Collection">Wallace Collection</a>, now damaged)</li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/Diana_and_Actaeon_(Titian)" title="Diana and Actaeon (Titian)">Diana and Actaeon</a></i>, owned jointly by the <a href="/wiki/National_Gallery" title="National Gallery">National Gallery</a> in London and the <a href="/wiki/National_Gallery_of_Scotland" class="mw-redirect" title="National Gallery of Scotland">National Gallery of Scotland</a> in Edinburgh</li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/Diana_and_Callisto" title="Diana and Callisto">Diana and Callisto</a></i>, were dispatched in 1559, owned jointly by the National Gallery and the National Gallery of Scotland</li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/The_Rape_of_Europa_(Titian)" title="The Rape of Europa (Titian)">The Rape of Europa</a></i> (Boston, <a href="/wiki/Isabella_Stewart_Gardner_Museum" title="Isabella Stewart Gardner Museum">Isabella Stewart Gardner Museum</a>), delivered in 1562</li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/The_Death_of_Actaeon" title="The Death of Actaeon">The Death of Actaeon</a></i>, now in the National Gallery in London, begun in 1559 but worked on for many years and never completed or delivered<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>The poesie, except for <i><a href="/wiki/The_Death_of_Actaeon" title="The Death of Actaeon">The Death of Actaeon</a></i>, were brought together for the first time in nearly 500 years in an exhibition in 2020 and 2021 that travelled from the <a href="/wiki/National_Gallery" title="National Gallery">National Gallery</a> in London, to the <span title="Spanish-language text"><span lang="es" style="font-style: normal;"><a href="/wiki/Museo_del_Prado" title="Museo del Prado">Museo del Prado</a></span></span> in Madrid, to the <a href="/wiki/Isabella_Stewart_Gardner_Museum" title="Isabella Stewart Gardner Museum">Isabella Stewart Gardner Museum</a> in Boston, where it closed on January 2, 2022.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul class="gallery mw-gallery-traditional">
	<li class="gallerycaption">Titian's <i>poesie</i> series for Philip II</li>

		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 195px">
			<div class="thumb" style="width: 190px; height: 180px;"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Perseo_y_Andr%C3%B3meda,_por_Tiziano.jpg" class="mw-file-description" title="Perseus and Andromeda"><img alt="Perseus and Andromeda" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Perseo_y_Andr%C3%B3meda%2C_por_Tiziano.jpg/160px-Perseo_y_Andr%C3%B3meda%2C_por_Tiziano.jpg" decoding="async" width="160" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Perseo_y_Andr%C3%B3meda%2C_por_Tiziano.jpg/240px-Perseo_y_Andr%C3%B3meda%2C_por_Tiziano.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Perseo_y_Andr%C3%B3meda%2C_por_Tiziano.jpg/320px-Perseo_y_Andr%C3%B3meda%2C_por_Tiziano.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4779" data-file-height="4374" /></a></span></div>

		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 195px">

</p>
			</div>
		</li>
</ul>
<p>Another painting that apparently remained in his studio at his death, and has been much less well known until recent decades, is the powerful, even "repellent" <i><a href="/wiki/Flaying_of_Marsyas_(Titian)" title="Flaying of Marsyas (Titian)">Flaying of Marsyas</a></i> (<a href="/wiki/Krom%C4%9B%C5%99%C3%AD%C5%BE" title="Kroměříž">Kroměříž</a>, <a href="/wiki/Czech_Republic" title="Czech Republic">Czech Republic</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Another violent masterpiece is <i><a href="/wiki/Tarquin_and_Lucretia" title="Tarquin and Lucretia">Tarquin and Lucretia</a></i> (<a href="/wiki/Cambridge" title="Cambridge">Cambridge</a>, <a href="/wiki/Fitzwilliam_Museum" title="Fitzwilliam Museum">Fitzwilliam Museum</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tizian_085.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Tizian_085.jpg/220px-Tizian_085.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="193" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Tizian_085.jpg/330px-Tizian_085.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Tizian_085.jpg/440px-Tizian_085.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2281" data-file-height="2000" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/The_Rape_of_Europa_(Titian)" title="The Rape of Europa (Titian)"><i>The Rape of Europa</i></a> c.&#160;1560–1562, <a href="/wiki/Isabella_Stewart_Gardner_Museum" title="Isabella Stewart Gardner Museum">Isabella Stewart Gardner Museum</a>, is a bold diagonal composition that <a href="/wiki/Peter_Paul_Rubens" title="Peter Paul Rubens">Rubens</a> admired and copied. In contrast to the clarity of Titian's early works, it is almost baroque in its blurred lines, swirling colours, and vibrant brushstrokes.</figcaption></figure>
<p>For each problem he undertook, he furnished a new and more perfect formula. He never again equaled the emotion and tragedy of <i><a href="/wiki/The_Crowning_with_Thorns_(Titian,_Paris)" title="The Crowning with Thorns (Titian, Paris)">The Crowning with Thorns</a></i> (Louvre); in the expression of the mysterious and the divine he never equaled the poetry of the <i>Pilgrims of Emmaus</i>; while in superb and heroic brilliancy he never again executed anything more grand than <i>The Doge Grimani adoring Faith</i> (Venice, Doge's Palace), or the <i>Trinity</i>, of Madrid. On the other hand, from the standpoint of flesh tints, his most moving pictures are those of his old age, such as the <i>poesie</i> and the <i>Antiope</i> of the Louvre. He even attempted problems of <a href="/wiki/Chiaroscuro" title="Chiaroscuro">chiaroscuro</a> in fantastic night effects (<i>Martyrdom of St. Laurence</i>, Church of the Jesuits, Venice; <i>St. Jerome</i>, Louvre; <a href="/wiki/Crucifixion_(Titian)" title="Crucifixion (Titian)">Crucifixion</a>, Church of San Domenico, Ancona).
</p><p>Titian had engaged his daughter Lavinia, the beautiful girl whom he loved deeply and painted various times, to Cornelio Sarcinelli of Serravalle. She had succeeded her aunt Orsa, then deceased, as the manager of the household, which, with the lordly income that Titian made by this time, placed her on a corresponding footing. Lavinia's marriage to Cornelio took place in 1554. She died in childbirth in 1560.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111024_27-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111024-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Titian was at the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Trent" title="Council of Trent">Council of Trent</a> towards 1555, of which there is a finished sketch in the Louvre. His friend Aretino died suddenly in 1556, and another close intimate, the sculptor and architect <a href="/wiki/Jacopo_Sansovino" title="Jacopo Sansovino">Jacopo Sansovino</a>, in 1570. In September 1565 Titian went to Cadore and designed the decorations for the church at Pieve, partly executed by his pupils. One of these is a Transfiguration, another an <i><a href="/wiki/Annunciation_(church_of_San_Salvador)" title="Annunciation (church of San Salvador)">Annunciation</a></i> (now in San Salvatore, Venice), inscribed <i>Titianus fecit</i>, by way of protest (it is said) against the disparagement of some persons who caviled at the veteran's failing handicraft.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111024_27-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111024-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Around 1560,<sup id="cite_ref-BBC_Madonna_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC_Madonna-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Titian painted the oil on canvas <i><a href="/wiki/Madonna_and_Child_with_Saints_Luke_and_Catherine_of_Alexandria" title="Madonna and Child with Saints Luke and Catherine of Alexandria">Madonna and Child with Saints Luke and Catherine of Alexandria</a></i>, a derivative on the motif of <a href="/wiki/Madonna_(art)" title="Madonna (art)">Madonna and Child</a>. It is suggested that members of Titian's Venice workshop probably painted the curtain and Luke, because of the lower quality of those parts.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Accademia_-_Piet%C3%A0_by_Titian.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Accademia_-_Piet%C3%A0_by_Titian.jpg/220px-Accademia_-_Piet%C3%A0_by_Titian.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="223" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Accademia_-_Piet%C3%A0_by_Titian.jpg/330px-Accademia_-_Piet%C3%A0_by_Titian.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Accademia_-_Piet%C3%A0_by_Titian.jpg/440px-Accademia_-_Piet%C3%A0_by_Titian.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4854" data-file-height="4912" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Piet%C3%A0_(Titian)" title="Pietà (Titian)"><i>Pietà</i></a>, c.&#160;1576, his last painting. <a href="/wiki/Gallerie_dell%27Accademia" title="Gallerie dell&#39;Accademia">Gallerie dell'Accademia</a>, Venice.</figcaption></figure>
<p>He continued to accept commissions to the end of his life. Like many of his late works, Titian's last painting, the <a href="/wiki/Piet%C3%A0_(Titian)" title="Pietà (Titian)"><i>Pietà</i></a>, is a dramatic, nocturnal scene of suffering. He apparently intended it for his own tomb chapel. He had selected, as his burial place, the chapel of the Crucifix in the Basilica di Santa Maria Gloriosa dei Frari, the church of the Franciscan Order. In payment for a grave, he offered the <a href="/wiki/Franciscan" class="mw-redirect" title="Franciscan">Franciscans</a> a picture of the <a href="/wiki/Piet%C3%A0_(Titian)" title="Pietà (Titian)">Pietà</a> that represented himself and his son Orazio, with a <a href="/wiki/Sibyl" title="Sibyl">sibyl</a>, before the Savior. He nearly finished this work, but differences arose regarding it, and he settled on being interred in his native Pieve.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111024_27-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111024-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Titian&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Frari_(Venice)_nave_right_-_Monument_of_Titian.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Frari_%28Venice%29_nave_right_-_Monument_of_Titian.jpg/220px-Frari_%28Venice%29_nave_right_-_Monument_of_Titian.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="191" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Frari_%28Venice%29_nave_right_-_Monument_of_Titian.jpg/330px-Frari_%28Venice%29_nave_right_-_Monument_of_Titian.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Frari_%28Venice%29_nave_right_-_Monument_of_Titian.jpg/440px-Frari_%28Venice%29_nave_right_-_Monument_of_Titian.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5546" data-file-height="4813" /></a><figcaption>Tomb of Titian in <a href="/wiki/Santa_Maria_Gloriosa_dei_Frari" title="Santa Maria Gloriosa dei Frari">Santa Maria Gloriosa dei Frari</a>, Venice</figcaption></figure>
<p>While the <a href="/wiki/Bubonic_plague" title="Bubonic plague">plague</a> raged in Venice, Titian died on 27 August 1576.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Depending on his unknown birthdate (see above), he was somewhere from his late eighties or even close to 100. Titian was interred in the Frari (Basilica di <a href="/wiki/Santa_Maria_Gloriosa_dei_Frari" title="Santa Maria Gloriosa dei Frari">Santa Maria Gloriosa dei Frari</a>), as at first intended, and his <i>Pietà</i> was finished by <a href="/wiki/Palma_il_Giovane" title="Palma il Giovane">Palma il Giovane</a>. He lies near his own famous painting, the <i>Madonna di Ca' Pesaro.</i> No memorial marked his grave. Much later the Austrian rulers of Venice commissioned <a href="/wiki/Antonio_Canova" title="Antonio Canova">Antonio Canova</a> to sculpt the large monument still in the church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111024_27-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111024-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Very shortly after Titian's death, his son, assistant and sole heir <a href="/wiki/Orazio_Vecellio" title="Orazio Vecellio">Orazio</a>, also died of the plague, greatly complicating the settlement of his estate, as he had made no will.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Printmaking">Printmaking</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Titian&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Printmaking">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Titian_-_Drowning_of_the_Pharaoh%27s_Host_in_the_Red_Sea_-_WGA22989.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/Titian_-_Drowning_of_the_Pharaoh%27s_Host_in_the_Red_Sea_-_WGA22989.jpg/220px-Titian_-_Drowning_of_the_Pharaoh%27s_Host_in_the_Red_Sea_-_WGA22989.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="118" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/Titian_-_Drowning_of_the_Pharaoh%27s_Host_in_the_Red_Sea_-_WGA22989.jpg/330px-Titian_-_Drowning_of_the_Pharaoh%27s_Host_in_the_Red_Sea_-_WGA22989.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/Titian_-_Drowning_of_the_Pharaoh%27s_Host_in_the_Red_Sea_-_WGA22989.jpg/440px-Titian_-_Drowning_of_the_Pharaoh%27s_Host_in_the_Red_Sea_-_WGA22989.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1247" data-file-height="670" /></a><figcaption><i>Drowning of Pharaoh's Army in the Red Sea</i>, 1515-17, woodcut, 221.5 cm wide</figcaption></figure>
<p>Titian never attempted <a href="/wiki/Engraving" title="Engraving">engraving</a>, but he was very conscious of the importance of <a href="/wiki/Printmaking" title="Printmaking">printmaking</a> as a means to expand his reputation. In the period 1515–1520 he designed a number of <a href="/wiki/Woodcut" title="Woodcut">woodcuts</a>, including an enormous and impressive one of <i>The Drowning of Pharaoh's Army in the Red Sea</i>, in twelve blocks, intended as wall decoration as a substitute for paintings;<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> and collaborated with <a href="/wiki/Domenico_Campagnola" title="Domenico Campagnola">Domenico Campagnola</a> and others, who produced additional <a href="/wiki/Old_master_print" title="Old master print">prints</a> based on his paintings and drawings. Much later he provided drawings based on his paintings to <a href="/wiki/Cornelis_Cort" title="Cornelis Cort">Cornelis Cort</a> from the Netherlands who engraved them. <a href="/wiki/Martino_Rota" title="Martino Rota">Martino Rota</a> followed Cort from about 1558 to 1568.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Painting_materials">Painting materials</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Titian&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Painting materials">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Titian employed an extensive array of pigments and it can be said that he availed himself of virtually all available pigments of his time.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> In addition to the common pigments of the Renaissance period, such as <a href="/wiki/Ultramarine" title="Ultramarine">ultramarine</a>, <a href="/wiki/Vermilion" title="Vermilion">vermilion</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lead-tin-yellow" class="mw-redirect" title="Lead-tin-yellow">lead-tin yellow</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ochres" class="mw-redirect" title="Ochres">ochres</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Azurite" title="Azurite">azurite</a>, he also used the rare pigments <a href="/wiki/Realgar" title="Realgar">realgar</a> and <a href="/wiki/Orpiment" title="Orpiment">orpiment</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Family_and_workshop">Family and workshop</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Titian&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Family and workshop">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Titian_-_Allegorie_der_Zeit.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/Titian_-_Allegorie_der_Zeit.jpg/220px-Titian_-_Allegorie_der_Zeit.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="246" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/Titian_-_Allegorie_der_Zeit.jpg/330px-Titian_-_Allegorie_der_Zeit.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/Titian_-_Allegorie_der_Zeit.jpg/440px-Titian_-_Allegorie_der_Zeit.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3782" data-file-height="4226" /></a><figcaption><i><a href="/wiki/Allegory_of_Prudence" title="Allegory of Prudence">The Allegory of Age Governed by Prudence</a></i> (c.&#160;1565–1570) is thought to depict (from left) Titian, his son <a href="/wiki/Orazio_Vecellio" title="Orazio Vecellio">Orazio</a>, and his nephew, <a href="/wiki/Marco_Vecellio" title="Marco Vecellio">Marco Vecellio</a>. <a href="/wiki/National_Gallery" title="National Gallery">National Gallery</a>, London.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Titian's wife, Cecilia, was a barber's daughter from his hometown village of <a href="/wiki/Cadore" title="Cadore">Cadore</a>. As a young woman she had been his housekeeper and mistress for some five years. Cecilia had already borne Titian two fine sons, Pomponio and <a href="/wiki/Orazio_Vecellio" title="Orazio Vecellio">Orazio</a>, when in 1525 she fell seriously ill. Titian, wishing to legitimize the children, married her. Cecilia recovered, the marriage was a happy one, and they had another daughter who died in infancy.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> In August 1530 Cecilia died. Titian remarried, but little information is known about his second wife; she was possibly the mother of his daughter Lavinia.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Titian had a fourth child, Emilia, the result of an affair, possibly with a housekeeper.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> His favourite child was Orazio, who became his assistant.
</p><p>In August 1530, Titian moved his two sons and infant daughter to a new home and convinced his sister Orsa to come from Cadore and take charge of the household. The mansion, difficult to find now, is in the Biri Grande, then a fashionable suburb, at the extreme end of Venice, on the sea, with beautiful gardens and a view towards <a href="/wiki/Murano" title="Murano">Murano</a>. In about 1526 he had become acquainted, and soon close friends, with <a href="/wiki/Pietro_Aretino" title="Pietro Aretino">Pietro Aretino</a>, the influential and audacious figure who features so strangely in the chronicles of the time. Titian sent a portrait of him to Gonzaga, duke of <a href="/wiki/Mantua" title="Mantua">Mantua</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111023_7-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111023-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When he was very young, the famed Italian painter <a href="/wiki/Tintoretto" title="Tintoretto">Tintoretto</a> was brought to Titian's studio by his father. This was supposedly around 1533, when Titian was (according to the ordinary accounts) over 40 years of age. Tintoretto had only been ten days in the studio when Titian sent him home for good, because the great master observed some very spirited drawings, which he learned to be the production of Tintoretto; it is inferred that he became at once jealous of so promising a student. This, however, is mere conjecture; and perhaps it may be fairer to suppose that the drawings exhibited so much independence of manner that Titian judged that young Jacopo, although he might become a painter, would never be properly a pupil. From this time forward the two always remained upon distant terms, though Tintoretto being indeed a professed and ardent admirer of Titian, but never a friend, and Titian and his adherents turned a cold shoulder to him. There was also active disparagement, but it passed unnoticed by Tintoretto.
</p><p>Several other artists of the Vecelli family followed in the wake of Titian. <a href="/wiki/Francesco_Vecellio" title="Francesco Vecellio">Francesco Vecellio</a>, his older brother, was introduced to painting by Titian (it is said at the age of twelve, but chronology will hardly admit of this), and painted in the church of S. Vito in Cadore a picture of the titular saint armed. This was a noteworthy performance, of which Titian (the usual story) became jealous; so Francesco was diverted from painting to soldiering, and afterwards to mercantile life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111025_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111025-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Titian_-_Diana_and_Actaeon_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Titian_-_Diana_and_Actaeon_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/280px-Titian_-_Diana_and_Actaeon_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" decoding="async" width="280" height="257" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Titian_-_Diana_and_Actaeon_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/420px-Titian_-_Diana_and_Actaeon_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Titian_-_Diana_and_Actaeon_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/560px-Titian_-_Diana_and_Actaeon_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3000" data-file-height="2750" /></a><figcaption><i><a href="/wiki/Diana_and_Actaeon_(Titian)" title="Diana and Actaeon (Titian)">Diana and Actaeon</a></i>, 1556–1559.<br /> <a href="/wiki/National_Galleries_of_Scotland" class="mw-redirect" title="National Galleries of Scotland">National Galleries of Scotland</a>, Edinburgh.</figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Marco_Vecellio" title="Marco Vecellio">Marco Vecellio</a>, called Marco di Tiziano, born in 1545, was Titian's nephew and was constantly with the master in his old age, and learned his methods of work. He has left some able productions in the ducal palace, the <i>Meeting of Charles V and <a href="/wiki/Pope_Clement_VII" title="Pope Clement VII">Clement VII</a> in 1529</i>; in <a href="/wiki/San_Giacomo_di_Rialto" title="San Giacomo di Rialto">San Giacomo di Rialto</a>, an <i>Annunciation</i>; in <a href="/wiki/Santi_Giovanni_e_Paolo,_Venice" title="Santi Giovanni e Paolo, Venice">Santi Giovanni e Paolo</a>, <i>Christ Fulminant</i>. A son of Marco, named Tiziano (or Tizianello), painted early in the 17th century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111025_50-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111025-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>From a different branch of the family came Fabrizio di Ettore, a painter who died in 1580. His brother Cesare, who also left some pictures, is well known by his book of engraved costumes, <i>Abiti antichi e moderni</i>. Tommaso Vecelli, also a painter, died in 1620. There was another relative, Girolamo Dante, who, being a scholar and assistant of Titian, was called <a href="/wiki/Girolamo_di_Tiziano" title="Girolamo di Tiziano">Girolamo di Tiziano</a>. Various pictures of his were touched up by the master, and are difficult to distinguish from originals.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERossetti19111025_50-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERossetti19111025-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Few of the pupils and assistants of Titian became well known in their own right; for some being his assistant was probably a lifetime career. <a href="/wiki/Paris_Bordone" title="Paris Bordone">Paris Bordone</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bonifazio_Veronese" title="Bonifazio Veronese">Bonifazio Veronese</a> were his assistants during at some point in their careers. <a href="/wiki/Giulio_Clovio" title="Giulio Clovio">Giulio Clovio</a> said Titian employed <a href="/wiki/El_Greco" title="El Greco">El Greco</a> (or Dominikos Theotokopoulos) in his last years. <a href="/wiki/Polidoro_da_Lanciano" title="Polidoro da Lanciano">Polidoro da Lanciano</a> is said to have been a follower or pupil of Titian. Other followers were <a href="/wiki/Nadalino_da_Murano" title="Nadalino da Murano">Nadalino da Murano</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Damiano_Mazza_(artist)" title="Damiano Mazza (artist)">Damiano Mazza</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> and Gaspare Nervesa.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Present_day">Present day</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Titian&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Present day">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:0_Portrait_d%27une_femme_%C3%A0_sa_toilette_-_Titien_-_Louvre_(INV_755)_-_(2).JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/0_Portrait_d%27une_femme_%C3%A0_sa_toilette_-_Titien_-_Louvre_%28INV_755%29_-_%282%29.JPG/220px-0_Portrait_d%27une_femme_%C3%A0_sa_toilette_-_Titien_-_Louvre_%28INV_755%29_-_%282%29.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="275" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/0_Portrait_d%27une_femme_%C3%A0_sa_toilette_-_Titien_-_Louvre_%28INV_755%29_-_%282%29.JPG/330px-0_Portrait_d%27une_femme_%C3%A0_sa_toilette_-_Titien_-_Louvre_%28INV_755%29_-_%282%29.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/0_Portrait_d%27une_femme_%C3%A0_sa_toilette_-_Titien_-_Louvre_%28INV_755%29_-_%282%29.JPG/440px-0_Portrait_d%27une_femme_%C3%A0_sa_toilette_-_Titien_-_Louvre_%28INV_755%29_-_%282%29.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2232" data-file-height="2786" /></a><figcaption><i><a href="/wiki/Woman_with_a_Mirror" title="Woman with a Mirror">Woman with a Mirror</a></i>, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1515</span>, <a href="/wiki/Louvre" title="Louvre">Louvre</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Contemporary estimates attribute around 400 works to Titian, of which about 300 survive.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Two of Titian's works in private hands were put up for sale in 2008. One of these, <a href="/wiki/Diana_and_Actaeon_(Titian)" title="Diana and Actaeon (Titian)"><i>Diana and Actaeon</i></a>, was purchased by the <a href="/wiki/National_Gallery" title="National Gallery">National Gallery</a> in London and the <a href="/wiki/National_Galleries_of_Scotland" class="mw-redirect" title="National Galleries of Scotland">National Galleries of Scotland</a> on 2 February 2009 for £50&#160;million.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> The galleries had until 31 December 2008 to make the purchase before the work would be offered to private collectors, but the deadline was extended. The sale created controversy with politicians who argued that the money could have been spent more wisely during a deepening recession. The <a href="/wiki/Scottish_Government" title="Scottish Government">Scottish Government</a> offered £12.5&#160;million and £10&#160;million came from the <a href="/wiki/National_Heritage_Memorial_Fund" title="National Heritage Memorial Fund">National Heritage Memorial Fund</a>. The rest of the money came from the National Gallery and from private donations.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> The other painting, <i>Diana and Callisto</i>, was for sale for the same amount until 2012 before it was offered to private collectors.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Titian_hair">Titian hair</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Titian&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Titian hair">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p><a href="/wiki/Titian_hair" title="Titian hair">Titian hair</a> has been used to describe red hair, almost always on women, since the 19th century.  <a href="/wiki/Anne_Shirley" title="Anne Shirley">Anne Shirley</a>, from <a href="/wiki/Lucy_Maud_Montgomery" title="Lucy Maud Montgomery">Lucy Maud Montgomery</a>'s 1908 novel <i><a href="/wiki/Anne_of_Green_Gables" title="Anne of Green Gables">Anne of Green Gables</a></i>, is described as having Titian hair when 15:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Well, we heard him say—didn't we, Jane?—'Who is that girl on the platform with the splendid Titian hair? She has a face I should like to paint.' There now, Anne. But what does Titian hair mean?"
"Being interpreted it means plain red, I guess," laughed Anne. "Titian was a very famous artist who liked to paint red-haired women."<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup></p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite><a href="/wiki/Lucy_Maud_Montgomery" title="Lucy Maud Montgomery">L. M. Montgomery</a>, Anne of Green Gables</cite></div></blockquote>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Titian&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist">
<div class="mw-references-wrap mw-references-columns"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-1"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-1">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">See below; <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1488/1490</span> is generally accepted despite claims in his lifetime that he was older, <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.getty.edu/vow/ULANFullDisplay?find=Titian&amp;role=&amp;nation=&amp;prev_page=1&amp;subjectid=500031075">Getty Union Artist Name List</a> and <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.metmuseum.org/toah/hd/tita/hd_tita.htm">Metropolitan Museum of Art timeline, retrieved 11 February 2009</a> both use c.&#160;1488. See discussion of the issue below and at <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://web.archive.org/web/20030520094844/http://lafrusta.homestead.com/riv_tiziano.html">When Was Titian Born?</a>, which sets out the evidence, and supports 1477—an unusual view today. Gould (pp. 264–66) also sets out much of the evidence without coming to a conclusion. Charles Hope in Jaffé (p. 11) also discusses the issue, favouring a date "in or just before 1490" as opposed to the much earlier dates, as does Penny (p. 201) "probably in 1490 or a little earlier". The question has become caught up in the still controversial division of works between <a href="/wiki/Giorgione" title="Giorgione">Giorgione</a> and the young Titian.</span>
</li>

</li>
